018006:  *                program. Fail toward the safe side.

006463: /* ONE OWNER FOR HISTORY→DAY IDENTITY, applied at load rather than at every reader.
006464:  *
006465:  * v578 made day ids deterministic and added `historyDayIndex` so pre-v578 logs — which carry random
006466:  * ids matching nothing — could still be placed by their day LABEL. That was the right resolution but
006467:  * the wrong SHAPE: a runtime resolver has to be adopted by every reader, and SEVEN read history by
006468:  * dayId. v578 converted three (the rotation cursor, the schedule lookup, the plan tab's completed
006469:  * markers) and left the rest, so `recentDayPerf` silently dropped legacy sessions from its per-day
006470:  * anchor, the session-length calibrator skipped them, and plateau scoping survived only because it
006471:  * happens to fall back when a lift has been trained on a single day. That is precisely the failure
006472:  * Haiden pointed at in the self-test: a rule updated in one place and not the others.
006473:  *
006474:  * So the resolution happens ONCE, at the boundary, exactly where `normalizeGyms` already does the
006475:  * same job for gyms. After this runs, every reader — including ones nobody has written yet — sees a
006476:  * dayId that resolves, and none of them needs to know legacy ids ever existed.
006477:  *
006478:  * IN MEMORY, NOT WRITTEN BACK. The store is left untouched; this shapes what the app holds. A boot

014392: // --- learned recovery ------------------------------------------------------------------------
014393: // The clock model (30 + sets·2.3h) is a population average; actual recovery is individual and muscle-
014394: // specific. This MEASURES it from the lifter's own history: performance-on-repeat. Each time a muscle
014395: // was retrained, pair the rest gap (hours since it was last trained) with the performance ratio (this
014396: // session's best e1RM ÷ the previous session's). Retrain before you've recovered and performance dips;
014397: // once recovered it holds — so a least-squares line ratio = a + b·gap identifies the rest at which
014398: // performance returns to the prior level, i.e. the recovery time. Shrunk toward the clock model by
014399: // confidence, clamped to a sane [18,120]h band, memoized on (history,part).
014400: //
014401: // Deliberately one-directional and conservative: it will RAISE recovery time for a lifter who
014402: // demonstrably dips on short rest (b>0, intercept<1), which is the case that matters — it protects a
014403: // slow-recovering muscle from being retrained (or trimmed) too eagerly. It stays silent (returns null →
014404: // clock model) when rest doesn't measurably help (b≈0), because "always fine at every gap I've tried"
014405: // cannot honestly prove a FASTER-than-clock recovery — only the absence of a dip, which isn't the same
014406: // thing. Identifiability floor: ≥8 repeat observations and ≥24h of gap spread, or it returns null.
014407: const __recovFitMemo = new WeakMap();

014414:         __recovFitMemo.set(history, byPart);
014415:     }

030863: /* LONGHAND BORDERS ON PURPOSE. These style objects are SPREAD into call sites that then override a
030864:    single border part — `{ ...iconBtn(), borderColor: C.accent }` is the common shape, and the
030865:    focused-mode toggle overrides it CONDITIONALLY. Mixing a `border` shorthand with a longhand that
030866:    changes between renders makes React warn that the result can be wrong, and it genuinely can: which
030867:    one wins depends on property order. Emitting the three longhands keeps every override well-defined.
030868:    Do not "tidy" these back into a `border:` shorthand. */
030869: const iconBtn = () => ({ width: 38, height: 38, borderRadius: 12, borderWidth: 1, borderStyle: "solid", borderColor: C.border, background: C.card, color: C.text, display: "flex", alignItems: "center", justifyContent: "center", cursor: "pointer", flexShrink: 0 });

032922: // The app shell (index.html, app.js, app.css) is served cache-first by the service worker for
032923: // instant loads and full offline support — which also means a shipped code fix can sit on the
032924: // server indefinitely without ever reaching an already-visited device until the normal
032925: // detect-new-worker → "Restart" flow completes. If that flow itself never gets a chance to run (the
032926: // tab stays open, the update check hasn't fired yet, or an EARLIER cached version had a bug in the
032927: // update-checker itself), there was previously no way out of a stale build short of digging through
032928: // the browser's OS-level site-data settings — which also wipes localStorage, taking workout history
032929: // and saved programs down with it. This is the safe, in-app escape hatch: it clears only the
032930: // service worker's registration and its CacheStorage entries (a completely separate storage system
032931: // from localStorage), then reloads — the freshly-registered worker re-fetches everything from the
032932: // network, guaranteeing the latest deployed build. History, programs, and settings are untouched.

034178:     /* Every write to a syncable list goes through here, so `updatedAt` and tombstones are recorded in ONE
034179:      * place rather than at each of the ~40 call sites that mutate these lists — where the next one added
034180:      * would silently forget to, and a forgotten stamp is a record that loses every merge it ever enters.
034181:      *
034182:      * Detecting what changed is free because the app already updates immutably: a record that came out of
034183:      * the updater with the same object identity it went in with was not touched, and anything else is new
034184:      * or edited and gets stamped. Ids present before and gone after are deletions, and get a tombstone.
034185:      * Undo works without special handling — it re-adds the record, which stamps it with a time later than
034186:      * its own tombstone, and a tombstone only bites when it is newer than the record it points at.
034187:      */
034188:     const stampedSetter = (setRaw) => (updater) => setRaw(prev => {
034189:         const next = typeof updater === "function" ? updater(prev) : updater;
034190:         if (!Array.isArray(next) || !Array.isArray(prev))
034191:             return next;
034192:         const now = Date.now();
034193:         const before = new Map(prev.map(r => [r && r.id, r]));
034194:         const gone = new Set(prev.map(r => r && r.id));
034195:         next.forEach(r => { if (r)
034196:             gone.delete(r.id); });
034197:         if (gone.size)
034198:             setTombs(t => { const out = { ...t }; gone.forEach(id => { if (id != null)
034199:                 out[id] = now; }); return out; });
034200:         return next.map(r => (r && r.id != null && before.get(r.id) !== r) ? { ...r, updatedAt: now } : r);

017955:             out.history = d.history.map(stamp);
017956:         return out;
017957:     },
017958:     // 6 → 7: groundwork for merging two devices' data. Two things were missing and neither can be
017959:     // reconstructed after the fact, which is why they go in now rather than when a sync backend lands:
017960:     //
017961:     //   `tombs`  — deletions. A merge that only unions records can never delete anything: remove a
017962:     //              program on your phone, merge with the tablet that still has it, and it comes back
017963:     //              from the dead. A deletion has to be a FACT that travels, not an absence.
017964:     //   updatedAt — which of two versions of the same program is the newer one. Without it, a merge
017965:     //              has to guess, and guessing about someone's training data is not acceptable.
017966:     //
017967:     // Existing records get updatedAt = 0: any edit on any device beats a record that predates the
017968:     // concept, which is the only safe default (it can never cause a newer edit to lose).
017969:     7: (d) => {
017970:         const stamp = (x) => (x && typeof x === "object" && x.updatedAt == null) ? { ...x, updatedAt: 0 } : x;
017971:         const out = { ...d, tombs: d.tombs && typeof d.tombs === "object" ? d.tombs : {} };
017972:         if (Array.isArray(d.saved))
017973:             out.saved = d.saved.map(stamp);
017974:         if (Array.isArray(d.cycles))
017975:             out.cycles = d.cycles.map(stamp);
017976:         if (Array.isArray(d.custom))
017977:             out.custom = d.custom.map(stamp);
017978:         return out;
017979:     }
017980: };
017981: /* ============================== MERGE ==============================
017982:  * Combining two copies of the store — from a backup file, or (later) from another device.
017983:  *
017984:  * The rule that matters: THIS FUNCTION NEVER LOSES A WORKOUT. Everything else is negotiable; a logged
017985:  * session is not. Import used to REPLACE, which is why restoring an old backup onto a phone you had
017986:  * kept using quietly destroyed everything logged in between. Merging is what makes a backup a safety
017987:  * net instead of a loaded gun.
017988:  *
017989:  * Per-record rules, chosen by what the record actually is:
017990:  *
017991:  *   history      union by id. A session is an immutable record of something that happened; two devices
017992:  *                can only ever hold different subsets of the truth, never conflicting versions of it.
017993:  *                Union is not a compromise here — it is the correct answer.
017994:  *   saved/cycles/custom
017995:  *                union by id, newest `updatedAt` wins. These are mutable documents, so a genuine

036666:     /* DELETE A WHOLE LOGGED SESSION. Haiden asked for this twice and remembered the app once having
036667:        it; it never did — I checked all 68 deployed builds, and what exists is "Delete entry" (ONE
036668:        exercise inside a log) and "Clear all". His two follow-on asks — that the rotation goes back a
036669:        day so he can redo it, and that fatigue resets — need no code of their own: both are DERIVED from
036670:        history rather than stored, so they follow the moment the entry is gone. The mirror is the part
036671:        that does not follow, which is what perfAfterDelete handles. */
036672:     const deleteHistoryEntry = (histId) => {
036673:         const gone = history.find(h => h.id === histId);
036674:         if (!gone)
036675:             return;
036676:         const prevHistory = history, prevPerf = perf;
036677:         setPerf(perfAfterDelete(history, perf, histId));
036678:         setHistory(prev => prev.filter(h => h.id !== histId));
036679:         /* UNDO, matching the "Clear all" precedent directly below. Deleting a workout is destructive and
036680:            restores BOTH halves — putting history back without the mirror would leave the app prescribing
036681:            from a session it no longer believes happened, which is the same split this function exists to
036682:            prevent, just in the other direction. */
036683:         setAppToast({
036684:             msg: `Deleted ${gone.dayLabel || "workout"}`,
036685:             undo: () => { setHistory(prevHistory); setPerf(prevPerf); setAppToast(null); }
036686:         });
036687:     };
